The continued investment in the Company’s natural gas infrastructure is needed 
3 
for Central Hudson to continue to provide safe, adequate, and reliable natural 
4 
gas service.  Over the five-year period commencing in 2024, approximately 62% 
5

6 
equipment.   The majority of this spending (52% or $213 million of the Five-Year 
7

8

9 
Company’s service territory.  This program also reduces the number of gas leaks 
10 
on the system which increases pipeline system safety and mitigates future 
11 
fugitive methane emissions.  Based solely on Annual Mass Emissions, sourced 
12 
from population factors from the EPA Subpart W reporting, the LPP replacement 
13 
program reduced  methane emissions by approximately 66% since 2016 and is 
14 
forecasted to deliver an additional 27% reduction by 2028. The Five-Year Gas 
15 
Capital Plan also budgets for customer requested service connections, totaling 
16 
$44.3 million over the Five-Year Gas Capital Plan.

GAS CAPITAL PROGRAM – “TRANSMISSION” 
5 
Q.    
What types of projects are funded in the “Transmission” category?  
6 
A.    
The category “Transmission” includes needed replacements and 
7 
modernization as well as new installations associated with the Company’s 
8 
gas transmission system that operates above 125 psig.  This is comprised 
9 
of gate and flow control stations, transmission pipelines, valves, and 
10 
associated appurtenances such as inline inspection device launchers and 
11 
receivers.  Noteworthy projects within this category include continued 
12 
replacement of transmission line valves with those which can 
13 
accommodate the installation of remote operators.  This benefits 
14 
customers by allowing for the rapid isolation of the affected portion of the 
15 
transmission system in the event of an unplanned breach of the 
16 
transmission line.  Additionally, these transmission line valves will be able 
17 
to accommodate In-Line Inspection (“ILI”) tools to expand the ability to 
18 
inspect the integrity of the transmission lines and comply with regulations.  
19 
Other significant projects within this category include replacement of the 
20 
aging Poughkeepsie Receival MP (Mahopac-Poughkeepsie) / TP

(Tuxedo-Poughkeepsie) interconnect station to allow for greater flexibility

4 
Q.    
What are the benefits of Line Valve Addition Program?  
5 
 
Adding additional line valves, specifically on the Transmission system, 
6 
improves public safety by allowing for a faster reaction to a pipeline 
7 
incident that would require sectionalizing the transmission system.  With 
8 
the implementation of the Intrepid Pipeline Integrity software, deficiencies 
9 
were noted with the spacing of Transmission line valves within Class 3 
10 
areas as prescribed per 16 NYCRR 255.179. These deficiencies were 
11 
identified due to population increases and the addition of new buildings 
12 
adjacent to the pipeline corridor.  This program would allow for the 
13 
addition of new transmission line valves to be installed with the future 
14 
capability to be automated/remote controlled and also accommodate In-
15 
Line Inspections as needed for integrity assessments.  
16

VI. 
GAS CAPITAL PROGRAM – “REGULATOR STATIONS” 
1 
Q.    
What types of projects are included in the “Regulator Stations” category? 
2 
A.    
The category “Regulator Stations” addresses needed replacement and 
3 
modernization of critical natural gas pressure control stations.  These 
4 
stations safely reduce transmission pressure to distribution or utilization 
5 
pressure.  Noteworthy projects within this category include replacement of 
6 
antiquated regulator stations which date to the time of manufactured gas 
7 
plants; pressure control upgrades and conditioning equipment to provide 
8 
enhanced safety and reliability; installation of coating systems to extend 
9 
the useful life of existing assets; and increased utilization of the 
10 
Company’s internal communication network system for monitoring system 
11 
pressures remotely and in real time for enhanced situational awareness.  
12 
Funding for this category totals $19.4 million in the Five-Year Gas Capital 
13 
Plan.   
14 
Q.    
How does the Company prioritize regulator station projects?  
15 
A.    
The Company uses a prioritization methodology to rank the regulator 
16 
stations and assign a risk score.  This risk score takes into account 
17 
different factors, including output pressure stability, feedback received 
18 
from the mechanics that work on the station, and antiquated/obsolete 
19 
equipment. Based on the risk score stations are prioritized and projects 
20 
are engineered to modernize and improve the stations.  These regulator 
21 
1206


--- Page 30 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
14 
 
station projects account for 80% of the Five-Year Gas Capital Plan in this 
1 
category.  
2 
Q.    
Why is output pressure stability important for prioritizing regulator station 
3 
projects?  
4 
A.    
Maintaining steady output pressure from regulator stations allows for 
5 
predictable performance of end use appliances.  This is critical for 
6 
utilization pressure systems, but also on upstream systems as well 
7 
because they are directly feeding utilization pressure systems and 
8 
elevated pressure customers. 
9 
Q.    
Please describe how antiquated and obsolete equipment factors into the 
10 
regulator station risk score.  
11 
A.    
Regulator station equipment is continuously maintained, once equipment 
12 
is not supported by the equipment manufacturer there is an increased risk 
13 
to the gas system by not having repair materials or support available in the 
14 
event of an equipment malfunction.  Typically, because equipment will 
15 
become unsupported by the manufacturer based on age of equipment, 
16 
station age is factored into the risk score.    
17 
Q.    
Is the Company adding any safety enhancements to Regulator Stations?   
18 
A.    
Yes, Central Hudson has recently improved the standard design for 
19 
regulator stations to increase resiliency and public safety by adding 
20 
additional safety devices for pressure control, a monitoring system which 
21 
is to be used in conjunction with a relief valve.  A monitoring system has 
22 
1207


--- Page 31 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
15 
 
two regulators.  One regulator is the operator or the unit that is controlling 
1 
the media flow into the downstream system.  The second regulator is the 
2 
monitor or standby regulator which will not come into operation until an 
3 
overpressure condition is experienced.  Beginning in 2023, all stations 
4 
being rebuilt will have two levels of overpressure protection, a relief valve 
5 
and a monitor regulator.  In these setups, the monitor regulators will be the 
6 
primary means of overpressure protection while the relief valve, located 50 
7 
feet away, will act as the secondary means of overpressure protection. In 
8 
the event of a worker regulator failure, the monitor regulator will take over 
9 
and an electronic alarm from the pressure recorders will notify the 
10 
appropriate emergency responder of a failure with no impact to 
11 
downstream customers.  This method will effectively reduce any 
12 
unintentional natural gas releases from overpressure events thereby 
13 
reducing greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emissions, while also enhancing 
14 
Regulator Station safety and resiliency. 
15 
Q.    
What impact does implementing monitor regulator overpressure protection 
16 
have on the overall budget for Regulator Stations?  
17 
 
Based on the first project in 2023 to have the increased pressure safety 
18 
equipment installed, this additional equipment will increase the overall 
19 
project budgets by approximately 10%.  This is due to additional monitor 
20 
regulator equipment, electronics for remote pressure monitoring, and 
21 
fabrication that is needed to accommodate the new robust design.   
22 
A. 
1208


--- Page 32 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
16 
 
Q.    
What other projects are included in the “Regulator Stations” category?  
1 
A.    
The Company is proposing a Regulator Station Coating Program.  
2 
Q.    
What is the Regulator Station Coating Program?  
3 
 
Central Hudson is proposing to commence a Transmission Regulator 
4 
Station Coating Program under the Five-Year Gas Capital Plan.  This 
5 
Program would match the Electric Transmission Structure Coating 
6 
Program, which is being proposed in the Electric Capital and Operations 
7 
Panel Testimony.  Due to the use of similar materials (carbon steel), the 
8 
benefits realized by the Electric Transmission Coating Program would 
9 
have a direct correlation to the transmission regulator station piping.  It is 
10 
expected recoating a station would extend the expected capital 
11 
replacement of the regulator station by 20 years.  The Transmission 
12 
Regulator Station Coating Program would recoat five stations per year 
13 
beginning July 1, 2024, utilizing existing coating condition and age as the 
14 
prioritization.  Looking at the Five-Year Gas Capital Plan, the Regulator 
15 
Station Coating Program is projected to spend $103,000 for 2024, 
16 
$265,000 for 2025, $269,000 for 2026, $279,000 for 2027, and $282,000 
17 
for 2028.  A summary of the cost benefit analysis and project prioritization 
18 
can be seen in Exhibit __ (GCOP-3).  As shown in the exhibit, the Coating 
19 
Program within the Five-Year Capital Plan will decrease future revenue 
20 
requirements and provides a positive net present value of approximately 
21 
$11.4 million.  Coating projects were prioritized based on the number of 
22 
A. 
1209


--- Page 33 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
17 
 
years since the stations last major overhaul and ranked based on station 
1 
coating condition.   
2 
Q.    
Why is this a capital program proposal when historically it has been an 
3 
expense task?  
4 
 
Recently, FERC issued an Order granting an accounting request for the 
5 
capitalization of a coating system for electric transmission structures.1 This 
6 
order grants a utility the ability to capitalize the replacement of a coating 
7 
system once over the life of the asset.  Transmission Regulator Stations 
8 
are made of the same material, Carbon Steel, and as such should be 
9 
treated the same.  If a station was recoated under expense previously, it 
10 
only included touch up paint which is not effective to postpone the 
11 
degradation of the surrounding coating.  Central Hudson has experienced 
12 
multiple stations in which the capital replacement of the station had to be 
13 
accelerated due to above grade corrosion.  If the stations had undergone 
14 
a capital coating replacement project these emergent replacements could 
15 
have been postponed. 
16

GAS CAPITAL PROGRAM – “NEW BUSINESS” 
17 
Q.    
What types of projects are identified in the “New Business” category?  
18 
 
The category “New Business” provides funding for tariff and customer 
19 
driven requests for new natural gas service.  Types of projects within this 
20 
 
1 https://elibrary.ferc.gov/eLibrary/filelist?accession_number=20220222-3055 
A.

PANEL 
18 
 
category include main and service extension to new natural gas 
1 
customers including both single residences or multiple family 
2 
developments such as townhouses, apartment buildings and underground 
3 
residential developments (“URD’s”) as well as those to serve new 
4 
commercial and industrial customers.  Funding levels are established 
5 
across the five-year period, to provide capital expenditures to support the 
6 
tariff driven, non-discretionary customer sales forecast.  Funding years 
7 
2024 and 2025 contain a cumulative incremental $5.2 million expenditure 
8 
associated with a large facility. 
9 
Q.    
What are the Panel’s observations regarding gas customer additions? 
10 
 
Customers continue to pursue natural gas to satisfy their energy needs.  
11 
While the Company offers and promotes alternatives such as heat pumps, 
12 
providing natural gas to customers is governed by tariff and is non-
13 
discretionary.  Expenditures in this category, however, could be impacted 
14 
by municipal, county, and state legislation, through building codes or 
15 
ordinances that could prevent the installation of new gas services in new 
16 
construction or other buildings.   
17 
Q.    
How is the Company proposing to address these potential impacts on the 
18 
New Business category? 
19 
 
Due to the non-discretionary nature of providing new service for customer 
20 
requests and our tariff obligation to render service, the Company is 
21 
A. 
A. 
1211


--- Page 35 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
19 
 
proposing that the New Business category of the capital budget have an 
1 
independent true-up mechanism.  
2 
Q.    
Please explain how the Company proposes the true-up mechanism to 
3 
work. 
4 
 
In the event that actual net plant for Gas New Business is under or over 
5 
the net plant target, which represents the amount approved in rates for 
6 
this category, the revenue requirement associated with this under/over 
7 
amount will be deferred for future pass back to or recovery from 
8 
customers.  Given the non-discretionary nature of this spending, 
9 
symmetrical deferral is appropriate; it provides customers with protection 
10 
should recently passed or future changes in legislation or code result in 
11 
the Company connecting less customers, while at the same time offering 
12 
protection to the Company for spending that is outside of their control and 
13 
is dictated by individual customer decisions.  Please also refer to the direct 
14

VIII. 
GAS CAPITAL PROGRAM – “DISTRIBUTION IMPROVEMENTS” 
1 
Q.    
What types of projects are included in the “Distribution Improvements” 
2 
category?  
3 
 
The category “Distribution Improvement” provides funding for gas 
4 
distribution main and service replacements as well as necessary cathodic 
5 
protection installations.  The types of projects are primarily a result of 
6 
Company identified elimination/replacement programs or municipal 
7 
infrastructure projects which are in conflict with the current location of the 
8 
Company’s natural gas facilities.  Funding for this category in the Five-
9 
Year Capital Plan is $301.9 million, with 71% being utilized for the LPP 
10 
programs, 20% for infrastructure improvements, and 9% for interference 
11 
work.  
12 
Q.    
Which identified elimination/replacement programs are the Company 
13 
currently engaged in?  
14 
 
Identified programs include the Company’s LPP Elimination Program and 
15 
Large Diameter Gas Welded Pipe Replacement Program.  LPP is pipe 
16 
materials with a significantly higher level of leakage per unit length than 
17 
plastic or steel that is monitored and protected against corrosion.  At the 
18 
present time, Central Hudson classifies LPP as cast iron, wrought iron, 
19 
and steel that is either bare or ineffectively coated.  Both programs’ 
20 
projects are reviewed and prioritized based on risk reduction and 
21 
operating experience.  The Company continues to eliminate LPP 
22 
A. 
A. 
1214


--- Page 38 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
22 
 
throughout its distribution system.  Significant portions of this pipe date 
1 
back to the original companies that formed Central Hudson in the early 
2 
1900s. A significant portion of LPP is located within the cities such as 
3 
Newburgh, Beacon, Poughkeepsie and Kingston, as well as the villages of 
4 
Saugerties and Catskill. At the end of 2022, the Company’s inventory of 
5 
leak prone main stood at 81.9 miles and 4,210 services.  Under the 
6 
current 2021 Rate Plan, the Company must eliminate at least 15 miles of 
7 
LPP per year. Forecasting based on continued elimination of 15 miles per 
8 
year results in a budget for 2024-2028 of $213 million.  
9 
Q.    
What is the Large Diameter Gas Welded Pipe Replacement Program?  
10 
 
It is a second replacement program that is funded separately from LPP 
11 
replacement program and targets large diameter gas welded steel pipe.  
12 
This pipe was typically installed in the early 1930s utilizing the 
13 
oxyacetylene welding process for joining segments of pipe.  The 
14 
Distribution Integrity Management Program (“DIMP”) has categorized this 
15 
construction method as having higher risk to the natural gas distribution 
16 
system and public safety, hence the replacement of this piping has been 
17 
prioritized along with LPP.  This project accounts for 4% of the Distribution 
18 
Improvements category.

Q.    
Please describe how the Company prioritizes the pipe replacement 
1 
projects?  
2 
 
Central Hudson ranks pipe replacement projects based on several factors.  
3 
In particular, Main Replacement Prioritization (“MRP”) software assigns 
4 
risk values to each pipeline segment based on leak history, pipeline 
5 
characteristics, population density, and many other parameters.  The 
6 
DIMP committee leads the review of the weighting factors utilized for the 
7 
risk value calculations in MRP.  Final pipeline project selection is done by 
8 
evaluating the results of MRP, and balancing them with planned 
9 
municipality work, LPP requirements under the current rate year, budget 
10 
constraints, and operating district need. 
11 
Q.    
Please provide a summary of the LPP requirements under the 2021 Rate 
12 
Plan. 
13 
 
The 2021 Rate Plan contains the LPP mileage elimination requirement of 
14 
15 miles per year beginning in 2022.  In addition, the prioritization of 
15 
identified projects must take pipe risk into account.    
16 
Q.    
Did Central Hudson meet the program elimination requirements in 2021 
17 
and 2022? 
18 
 
Yes, Central Hudson eliminated 20.1 miles in 2021 and 15.1 miles of LPP 
19 
in 2022 from its Pipeline and Hazardous Materials Safety Administration 
20 
(“PHMSA”) reported inventory.  The program reflected industry “best 
21 
practices” such as increasing average project sizes to 2 miles or more 
22

4

5 
 
The Company continues to identify means for reducing the costs 
6

8

9

12 
to proceeding.

17 
not met in a given calendar year.

The primary drivers of total costs of LPP work increasing greater than the 
1 
rate of inflation can be attributed to several factors, including ever 
2 
increasing qualification and training requirements.  Training and 
3 
qualification requirements have multiplied on an annual basis.  Gas 
4 
contractors must pay their workforce to become sufficiently trained and 
5 
qualified during times not working on a job.  Within Case 19-G-0736, the 
6 
Operator qualification program has undergone major changes, all 
7 
centered around additional training and QA/QC.  These increased 
8 
requirements are passed on and reflected in contractor bid pricing.  A 
9 
second factor is the inspector oversight work being performed.  The 
10 
QA/QC inspection program has expanded year-over-year with increased 
11 
oversight which results in increased costs.  Another driver for the increase 
12 
in proforma pricing is the cost of gas specific materials.  Gas specific 
13 
materials are heavily dependent on commodity costs.  The cost of 
14

17 
LPP main replacement program.  Currently these are not addressed until

Q.    
Are there other types of projects funded by the “Distribution 
1 
Improvements” category?  
2 
 
Other projects funded by this category include municipal infrastructure 
3 
projects such as water and sewer projects or highway relocations where 
4 
the planned municipal infrastructure (sewer, water, and drainage) is in 
5 
conflict with the location of the Company’s natural gas facilities also known 
6 
as interference work, both non-LPP and LPP.  The Company collaborates 
7 
closely with municipal officials in development of project scope 
8 
modifications that minimize the relocation or replacement of Company 
9 
facilities to the extent possible.  However, due to limited space for 
10 
underground facilities under streets and roads, these municipals 
11 
modernization projects result in conflicts with the existing gas 
12 
infrastructure.  This requires the relocation and replacement of Company 
13 
facilities.  These types of projects account for 9% of the Distribution 
14 
Improvements category.  
15 
Additionally, the Company is pursuing other main replacement programs 
16 
for reducing system risk, including the Creek Crossing Risk Remediation 
17 
project, the Compression Coupling Elimination Program, and the 
18 
Transmission Service Elimination Program.  
19 
Q.    
What is the Creek Crossing Risk Remediation Project?  
20 
 
Central Hudson’s network of piping traverses through many bodies of 
21 
water.  Central Hudson classifies these water crossings in two different 
22 
A. 
A. 
1223


--- Page 47 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
31 
 
ways, River Crossings and Creek Crossings. The River Crossings are the 
1 
five pipelines that traverse the Hudson River.  Other water body crossings 
2 
are considered Creek Crossings.   
3 
For the Creek Crossings, historical installation practices allowed for direct 
4 
laying, or shallow laying of pipelines within streams and creeks.  This, 
5 
combined with increased weather events involving substantial flooding, 
6 
water flow and erosion, poses a risk to Central Hudson.  For example, 
7 
Central Hudson has experienced multiple events in recent years in which 
8 
pipelines during abnormal weather events have become compromised 
9 
and created an emergency situation.  The Creek Crossing Risk 
10 
Remediation Project would proactively target creek crossings that pose a 
11 
high risk to the Company and install a bypass by either boring or rerouting 
12 
the pipeline strategically. The Company has identified 135 crossings.  
13 
Currently the Company plans to address a subset of these crossings 
14 
starting with a budget of $1.0 million in 2025, $1.4 million in 2026, $1.6 
15 
million in 2027 and $2.3 million in 2028.  Details can be found in 
16 
Confidential Exhibit __ (GCOP-6). 
17 
Q.    
Please describe the Compression Coupling Elimination Program. 
18 
 
During the 1940s through 1980s, non-leak prone pipe installations 
19 
(cathodically protected steel and plastic piping) utilized compression 
20 
couplings.  Compression couplings are impacted by the freeze/thaw 
21 
cyclical weather patterns that induces new stresses on the pipeline and 
22 
A. 
1224


--- Page 48 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
32 
 
can cause a leak.  This is an identified threat on the DIMP threat listing 
1 
since 2017 and is continuously reviewed.  Depending on its proximity to a 
2 
structure, operating pressure, and pipe size, these couplings can cause a 
3 
hazardous condition and contribute to GHG emissions.  Currently 
4 
compression coupling leaks continue to account for over 20% of the 
5 
annual leaks on Central Hudson’s distribution systems.  Details for this 
6 
proposed program can be found in Confidential Exhibit __ (GCOP-7).  
7 
Q.    
What is the proposed target of the Compression Coupling Elimination 
8 
Program? 
9 
 
The Company proposes a multi-year program, aimed at replacing 21.8 
10 
miles of main and approximately 2,000 services over five years starting in 
11 
2025 at a total five-year program cost of $9.3 million.  The replacement 
12 
targets are 0.6 miles of main in 2025, 1.0 miles of main in 2026, 1.4 miles 
13 
of main in 2027, and 1.4 miles of main in 2028.  The prioritization of this 
14 
program would focus on leak history.  
15 
Q.    
What is a Transmission Service?  
16 
 
A Transmission Service, also known as a Farm Tap, is gas service that is 
17 
being supplied from a transmission pipeline (supply pressure higher than 
18 
125psig).  These contain an above grade regulator set with a typical two 
19 
stage regulation as opposed to a traditional service which only requires 
20 
one stage regulation or none.  
21 
Q.    
Why is it desirable to reduce the number of Transmission Services? 
22 
A. 
A. 
1225


--- Page 49 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
33 
 
 
Although Transmission Services are still allowed by code, Central Hudson 
1 
has stopped installing them for residential and small commercial 
2 
customers.  Each Transmission Service carries with it a higher risk than 
3 
that of supplying gas from a distribution system.  In the event of pressure 
4 
regulation equipment failure or damage, there is higher risk of a 
5 
catastrophic event involving high pressure natural gas venting near a 
6 
house due to the higher potential volume of gas that could be released 
7 
from a Transmission Service compared to a gas service supplied from 
8 
distribution system.  Transmission Services therefore could pose a serious 
9 
risk to inhabitants and first responders.  Historically, Central Hudson has 
10 
opportunistically removed Transmission Services where possible, but the 
11 
Company is proposing a proactive program to relocate Transmission 
12 
Services to the distribution system.  
13 
Q.    
How many Transmissions Services are located within Central Hudson 
14 
service territory? 
15 
 
Central Hudson currently operates 158 Transmission Services. 
16 
Q.    
What is the proposal to reduce the number of Transmission Services?  
17 
 
A majority of the Farm Taps could be eliminated by installing distribution 
18 
main and service piping and supplying the customer from the distribution 
19 
system, or in some cases offering the customer an NPA solution.  Central 
20 
Hudson proposes to eliminate 20 Transmission Services in 2025, 20 
21 
services in 2026, and 20 services in 2027.  The replacement prioritization 
22 
A. 
A. 
A. 
1226


--- Page 50 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
34 
 
would be based on the risk and cost.  To reduce risk, the Company would 
1 
prioritize services that have the transmission pressure reduction at the 
2 
building wall or in close proximity to other houses.  Once projects are 
3 
prioritized, the Company will perform an NPA analysis on each project 
4 
before proceeding with a main/service installation project.  See 
5 
Confidential Exhibit __ (GCOP-8) for more details of the Transmission 
6 
Service Elimination Program.

GAS OPERATIONAL PROPOSALS 
1 
Q.    
Does the Company have any gas operational proposals? 
2 
 
Yes.  The Company is proposing additional Tree Canopy removal for Gas 
3 
Transmission Only Rights of Way (“ROW”). 
4 
Q.    
What is the current process for the tree canopy removal in Gas 
5 
Transmission ROW? 
6 
 
In instances where the Gas Transmission and overhead Electric systems 
7 
share the same corridors, the Electric Transmission and Distribution 
8 
(“T&D”) Department maintains the tree canopy.  The Company utilizes 
9 
approximately $100,000 for tree canopy trimming on Gas Only Right of 
10 
Ways and can maintain a linear length of 1.67 miles of ROW a year with 
11 
the present funding. 
12 
Q.    
Why is additional Tree Canopy Removal required? 
13 
 
Central Hudson has approximately 78 miles of Gas Transmission pipe 
14 
offroad and outside of the Electric ROW. These sections are in highly 
15 
forested areas that are difficult to visually inspect during the summer and 
16 
autumn months from an aerial patrol.  
17 
Q.    
Why is important to have a clear view of the ROW? 
18 
 
First and foremost, a clearer view of the ROW will reduce risk by making 
19 
the aerial visual inspection more effective. Following extreme weather 
20 
events like Hurricanes or large rain fall, Central Hudson will typically 
21 
deploy an emergency walking visual survey.  For an emergency walking 
22 
A. 
A. 
A. 
A. 
1228


--- Page 52 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND OPERATIONS 
PANEL 
36 
 
visual survey, the Company expects the costs to be between $16,000 to 
1 
$48,000 and will take anywhere from 1 week to 4 weeks to complete.  
2 
With a clear Gas ROW, the Company can perform a complete aerial patrol 
3 
of the transmission system in less than 1 day.  Aerial inspection provides a 
4 
broader perspective of actual or possible damages than what can be 
5 
achieved by walking in close proximity to the Transmission line. In 
6 
addition, the walking survey can present a safety risk for the personnel 
7 
walking especially if the ground is unsteady due to erosion.  A clear ROW 
8 
will also increase safety and security of the entire gas system by allowing 
9 
the Company to quickly identify additional threats like excavations, 
10 
encroachment, construction work, unauthorized activities, or evidence of 
11 
leaking natural gas.  
12 
Q.    
How will the tree canopy program be structured? 
13 
 
Central Hudson proposes a 12-year cyclical schedule for the Gas-only 
14 
ROW tree canopy program.  
15

What are the costs associated with a 12-year tree canopy removal cycle?

To implement a consistent and effective ROW management program, the 
17 
Company proposes to enhance the existing Vegetation management 
18 
system to include the new maintenance schedule and utilize walking leak 
19 
survey to identify target areas with overgrowth.  In addition, the Company 
20 
is seeking incremental funding for the Vegetation Management Software 
21 
system, labor for data entry, and the contract aerial and ground forces that

Q.    
Are there any additional initiatives that Gas Operations is pursuing?  
3 
 
Yes.  To fully comply with the PHMSA Gas Pipeline Mega Rule, additional 
4 
testing and inspections are needed.  With the creation and establishment 
5 
of Moderate Consequence Areas (“MCA”) coupled with the Assessments 
6 
of Pipelines Outside High Consequence Area (“HCA”) requirement, 
7 
pipeline integrity assessments have increased by approximately 300%.  
8 
With the additional 69 miles of pipeline testing being incorporated into the 
9 
integrity testing program from the new requirements, additional revenue 
10 
requirement would be needed to accommodate the robotic In-Line 
11 
Inspection (“ILI”) program. The robotic ILI program was created to inspect 
12 
pipeline sections that could not be inspected using traditional External 
13 
Corrosion Direct Assessment (“ECDA”) testing due to access issues.  
14 
These newly identified segments are identified after an ECDA survey is 
15 
accomplished in the nearby area and the tester reports an access issue 
16 
which would be a skip in recordable data.  The robotic ILI inspection would 
17 
then be scheduled for covering these segments as needed.  On average, 
18 
4 segments are identified a year, and each would have their own 
19 
associated validation digs to confirm ILI results. The cost associated with 
20 
these inspection and validation activities is estimated to be approximately 
21

4

13

15

16 
section 224-F (the “Act”).  Pursuant to the Act, as of September 15, 2023, 
17 
certain roadway construction work by utility company contractors and 
18 
subcontractors must comply with prevailing wage requirements.  
19 
Q.    
Will the Act impact the Company’s gas capital program?  
20 
A. 
Yes.  The Company, with the assistance of internal and external legal 
21 
counsel, reviewed the Act and the Updated Enforcement Guidance from

2

3

4

5

6

12

13

14

15

16

17 
expenditure level for the entire Transmission category, it indiscriminately 
18

19 
2024 and 2027, every project line item was cut by approximately 71%, and 
20 
for 2025 and 2026, every project line item was reduced by approximately 
21 
81%.  Tellingly, Staff neither eliminated nor deferred any specific project,

inconsistent” and “costs for line valve installation [is] missing data from

13 
and methodology provided by the Company, which is based on historical

expenditures for those types of projects.  In contrast, the Company’s 
15 
proforma estimates for the line valve projects are based on actual 
16

17

18

1250


--- Page 74 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND 
OPERATIONS PANEL 
18 
 
Q.    
Why does the Panel not agree with Staff’s revision to the proforma 
1 
pricing?  
2 
A. 
As required by Central Hudson’s work order authorization policy, initial 
3 
work order estimates must undergo revisions if there are any changes in 
4 
scope, assumptions, circumstances, or schedule.  Due to this 
5 
requirement, a work order often undergoes several revisions throughout 
6 
its life cycle, particularly during construction.  As seen in DPS-281 
7 
Attachment 1, included in Staff Exhibit__(SGIOP-1), Staff utilized work 
8 
order (project) estimates for the Leak Prone Replacement projects to 
9 
calculate annual differences.  The estimates provided in this IR response, 
10 
however, were not representative of initial work order revisions, but rather 
11 
later revisions that included project specific adjustments.  As a result, Staff 
12 
calculated an adjusted proforma pricing structure (utilizing a three-year 
13 
average) that resulted in a proforma adjustment of only 4.2%.  Contrary to 
14 
Staff’s method, initial work order revisions (estimates), should be used to 
15 
calculate proforma pricing in order to accurately reflect year-over-year 
16 
adjustments.  Utilizing the initial work order estimates as seen in Exhibit 
17 
__ (GCOP-3R), the Panel recalculated the differences, resulting in an 
18 
overall adjustment of 10.4%.   
19 
1251


--- Page 75 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE GAS CAPITAL AND 
OPERATIONS PANEL

3 
identified projects.  Proforma pricing should be used for all identified 
4 
projects, not limited to identified leak prone pipe projects, to capture a 
5 
more accurate budget.  Identified projects have an established scope and 
6 
include project specific footages and road types to be used with proforma 
7 
pricing.  Please refer to Confidential Exhibit __ (GCOP-4R) for project 
8 
details associated with these identified projects, including footages, road 
9 
types, and proforma pricing.

Q. 
Can you describe the process used for identifying and prioritizing the 
9 
technology investments within the Five-Year Capital Plan?  
10 
A. 
The Central Hudson Technology group collaborates and closely partners 
11 
with the 12 major areas responsible for Central Hudson operations to 
12 
create the prioritized portfolio of technology projects in the Five-Year 
13 
Capital Plan.  In 2022, the Technology group implemented an objective 
14 
prioritization framework to support the prioritization and selection of all 
15 
capital technology investments.  This framework, created in partnership 
16 
with the Company’s Technology Steering Committee (“TSC”) and Capital 
17 
Asset Review and Evaluation Committee (“CARE”), consists of six 
18 
1301


--- Page 125 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE TECHNOLOGY CAPITAL AND 
OPERATIONS PANEL 
8 
 
attributes derived from qualifying questions that are used to evaluate and 
1 
classify project requests.  These questions receive equal weighting when 
2 
determining the priority of a request and are as follows: 
3 
 Does the project satisfy or fulfill a regulatory requirement? 
4 
 Does the project result in cost savings, cost avoidance, and/or 
5 
revenue for Central Hudson or its stakeholders? 
6 
 Does the project enhance Central Hudson’s customer 
7 
experience? 
8 
 Does the project improve or enhance safety and wellness for 
9 
Central Hudson employees, contractors, or the public? 
10 
 Does the project reduce risk, debt, or vulnerabilities (i.e., legal, 
11 
cybersecurity, technology, infrastructure)? 
12 
 Is the project included in the current rate case authorization? 
13 
Projects are evaluated on a scale of zero to six based on the number of 
14 
attributes the investment satisfies, creating a priority score.  Each project 
15 
is then evaluated for relative urgency and classified as an ‘immediate’, 
16 
‘moderate’, or ‘low’ urgency initiative.  The priority and urgency ratings 
17 
combined with Central Hudson’s classifications of ‘maintaining standards’, 
18 
‘system enhancements’, and ‘non-discretionary’ support the global ranking 
19 
and sequencing of projects within the portfolio.  Project ranking is 
20 
leveraged to determine which projects receive capital funding. 
21 
1302


--- Page 126 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE TECHNOLOGY CAPITAL AND 
OPERATIONS PANEL 
9 
 
Additionally, project requests are evaluated to ensure overall alignment 
1 
with Technology strategic drivers.  These drivers are designed to ensure 
2 
that Central Hudson’s Technology investments align with industry best 
3 
practices, Central Hudson’s operational support capabilities, and 
4 
alignment with other factors such as risk and cost optimization: 
5 
 Cloud – this strategy supports the adoption of cloud technologies 
6 
for platforms whenever appropriate.  This approach aligns with 
7 
industry trends in that it minimizes software development and 
8 
infrastructure overhead, enables rapid scalability, enables 
9 
frequent and periodic software upgrades to provide additional 
10 
functionality and address security vulnerabilities. 
11 
 Mobility – this strategy focuses on equipping Central Hudson and 
12 
its customers with the ability to access data, applications, and 
13 
services anytime, anywhere, and from any-device with the proper 
14 
credentials.  The strategy supports mobile computing and 
15 
communication, while ensuring security, data privacy, and 
16 
compliance controls are in place.   
17 
 Sustainability, Reliability, & Rationalization – this strategy supports 
18 
efforts that enhance Central Hudson’s ability to sustain business 
19 
operations or improve the reliability of key technologies.  In 
20 
alignment with industry best practices, Central Hudson has an 
21 
obligation to continuously ensure that its technology assets are at 
22 
1303


--- Page 127 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE TECHNOLOGY CAPITAL AND 
OPERATIONS PANEL 
10 
 
current vendor-supported versions (N or N-1) to prevent 
1 
functionality and cybersecurity risks.  This strategy also focuses 
2 
on consolidating (minimizing) redundant legacy applications 
3 
whenever possible in favor of  centralized enterprise-wide 
4 
solutions. 
5 
 Cyber & Information Security – this strategy focuses on reducing 
6 
technical risk and vulnerabilities, supporting data privacy and 
7 
Central Hudson’s overall cybersecurity posture. 
8 
 Process Automation – this strategy aims to automate, streamline, 
9 
and optimize business processes wherever possible to reduce 
10 
errors, delays, and inefficiencies while increasing overall 
11 
productivity and potentially reducing cost. 
12 
Leaders from each of the 12 operational areas meet regularly as a 
13 
collective Technology Steering Committee to review and approve the 
14 
investments in the Technology portfolio in alignment with the strategic 
15 
plan.  The Technology Steering Committee, chaired by the Chief 
16 
Technology Officer, is also responsible for approving any changes to the 
17 
plan.  This prioritization process will continue to mature as incremental 
18 
improvements are made in the upcoming years.  The capital plan within 
19 
this testimony has been created and reviewed in accordance with this 
20 
process.

16 
implementing enhancements that will reduce the manual workload that 
17 
currently exists within the Business.  The SAP Dunning implementation 
18 
requires approximately $1 million of capital investment in the Rate Year.

replacement routers manufactured by an established, industry-
2 
leading vendor, and initiated an R&D project to test the 
3 
interoperability of the new routers in the current legacy router 
4 
network.  Central Hudson has developed a migration plan to phase 
5 
out the legacy equipment and replace it with new equipment.  This 
6 
investment aligns to the Technology strategic drivers of 
7 
Cybersecurity & Information Management and Sustainability & 
8 
Reliability.

Q.

13 
evaluates its staffing needs to determine where contractors or FTEs are 
14

6 
Q.

7 
Analytics capital funding within the Software category? 
8 
A. 
On page 21-22 of the SCP testimony, Staff agrees that “the costs of IEDR 
9 
Phase I and II projects should be treated as non-discretionary costs and 
10

A.

16 
I and II but disagrees with Staff’s recommendations for the IEDR – 
17 
Fin/Ops Data Analytics initiative.   
18 
Q.    
Please describe the portion of the IEDR Project called IEDR – Fin/Ops 
19 
Data Analytics. 
20 
A. 
The “IEDR – Fin/Ops Data Analytics” project modernizes the Company’s 
21 
data warehouse and reporting systems and consolidates legacy solutions 
22 
1365


--- Page 189 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE TECHNOLOGY CAPITAL AND 
OPERATIONS PANEL 
14 
 
with limited functionality onto the same scalable hosted solution used for 
1 
the state's IEDR program.  This initiative would also allow for ease of data 
2 
migration for future phases of IEDR as they are identified and capture 
3 
efficiencies since data sets migrated by the Data & Analytics project would 
4 
already be present within the new platform.  Merging the data solutions 
5 
will also improve the quality and accuracy of Company data by ensuring 
6 
that the same data governance policies and quality assurance tools are 
7 
applied to the common platform.  Finally, as data sets continue to grow in 
8 
scope and size, the project will also allow the Company to more easily 
9 
spin up additional servers and data storage to meet the requirements of 
10 
any IEDR or internal Company use cases. 
11 
Q.    
Why is Staff incorrect in stating that the IEDR – Fin/Ops Data Analytics 
12 
project should not be implemented along with the IEDR Phase I and II? 
13 
A. 
Central Hudson's proposed IEDR solution will entail the implementation of 
14 
a scalable cloud-hosted data lake to support the state's IEDR goals for 
15 
improved utility data sharing and transparency.  A data lake is a digital 
16 
reservoir that stores diverse raw data in its native format, allowing 
17 
organizations to later analyze and derive insights from this data in a 
18 
flexible and scalable manner.  If Central Hudson only implements the 
19 
cloud solution for the IEDR Phase I and II requirements, all other data sets 
20

should be revised.  In order to produce a fair reflection of a three-year

Q.    
What are the risks associated with long-term reliance on contractor Project 
13 
Management resources?   
14 
A. 
Long-term contractor staff augmentation poses several risks, including: 
15 
 Dependency on External Expertise: The Company’s historic 
16 
reliance on external contractors has created a dependency on 
17 
external expertise, stifling the Company’s ability to develop internal 
18 
1375


--- Page 199 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE TECHNOLOGY CAPITAL AND 
OPERATIONS PANEL 
24 
 
capabilities.  This has led to situations where critical knowledge 
1 
resides primarily with contractors, creating vulnerability if 
2 
contractors are not available or choose to leave.   
3 
 Lack of Institutional Knowledge: Due to their transitory nature, 
4 
Contractors may in certain circumstances lack in-depth knowledge 
5 
of the Company's specific processes, systems, and culture.  This 
6 
can lead to inefficiencies, misunderstandings, and delays in project 
7 
execution. 
8 
 Limited Commitment: Contractors may not have the same level of 
9 
commitment to the Company’s long-term goals as permanent 
10 
employees.  Contract staff tends to prioritize short-term gains and 
11 
may not be as invested in the long-term success and sustainability 
12 
of the projects they are involved in. 
13 
 Security and Compliance Risks:  Contractors could pose security 
14 
risks, especially if they have access to sensitive data and systems.  
15 
In many situations, IT projects may involve access to critical and 
16 
highly sensitive data or systems.  Ensuring compliance with 
17 
organizational policies, industry regulations, and security 
18 
standards becomes more challenging when relying heavily on 
19 
external resources.  
20

7 
implementation.  This challenge led to additional vendor costs and a 
8 
reliance on external vendors to take on the Company’s testing 
9 
responsibilities.  The Management Audit observes that testing is currently 
10 
completed by business resources within the business units.  These 
11 
resources are limited in number and are often constrained or 
12 
overallocated due to their full-time operational responsibilities.  These 
13 
resource constraints lead to project delays, which impact the Company’s 
14 
ability to keep pace with the demands of the capital portfolio and 
15 
operational system maintenance.  Many times, sourcing external testers is 
16 
not feasible on projects where detailed institutional knowledge about 
17 
organizational processes is required, particularly on projects involving 
18 
existing business service software.  The Testing/QA FTE will help to 
19 
alleviate business resource constraints by supplementing some of the 
20 
quality testing necessary on business service projects as well as equip the 
21

Q.    
Does the Company agree with Staff’s recommendation regarding Security 
1 
Reporting? 
2 
A. 
Only in part.  While the Company agrees that greater partnership with the 
3 
PSC is a positive endeavor, a requirement to report on FTEs or 
4 
organizational changes (at any level) is unnecessary and burdensome.  
5 
Staff’s requested requirement that the Company report on any project 
6 
updates, changes, or milestones is also unnecessary.  It is unclear what 
7 
value these administrative functions would provide.  In addition, the 
8 
documented reports could expose vulnerabilities for the Company and/or 
9 
customers should the reports be inadvertently released to the public by 
10 
either party or fall into the hands of malicious cybersecurity actors.  The 
11 
proposed Staff requirements would also impose additional administrative 
12 
overheads and costs as a result of the time associated with creating and 
13 
maintaining the reports.  Instead of creating more “reports,” the Company 
14 
proposes that the new Manager (Director) in Cybersecurity have a 
15 
standing monthly call with the PSC to provide a cybersecurity update 
16 
where projects and organizational structure updates can be fully 
17 
discussed.  Additionally, the Company will serve as Chair for the New 
18 
York State Security Working Group, which further affords greater 
19 
collaboration and a secure level of transparency into Security operations 
20 
at Central Hudson.

II. 
PURPOSE OF TESTIMONY 
3 
Q.    
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony in these proceedings? 
4 
A. 
The Panel addresses Central Hudson’s existing and proposed EAMs for 
5 
calendar years 2025 through 2027.  First, the Panel will discuss the history 
6 
and rationale for EAMs, which are being filed in this proceeding in 
7 
accordance with the Commission’s Order Adopting a Ratemaking and 
8 
Utility Revenue Model Policy Framework (“REV Track 2 Order”), issued 
9 
May 19, 2016, in Case 14-M-0101.  Next, the Panel will discuss the new 
10 
Disadvantaged Communities Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs being 
11 
proposed.  Then, the Panel will discuss the continuation of the current 
12 
Load Factor, DER Utilization, EV Adoption, EVSE&I DC Fast Charger and 
13 
Level 2, Electric and Gas Peak Reduction, and Residential and 
14 
Commercial Energy Intensity EAMs.  Finally, the Panel will address cost 
15 
recovery provisions. 
16 
Q.    
Is the Panel sponsoring any exhibits in support of its testimony? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  This Panel is sponsoring the following exhibits that were prepared by 
18 
or under the direction of the Panel or one of its members: 
19 
Exhibit __ (EAMP-1) contains the results of a benefit-cost analysis 
20 
associated with the Company’s proposed EAMs; 
21

III. 
RATIONALE FOR EAMs 
8 
Q.    
What are EAMs? 
9 
A. 
EAMs are designed to incentivize the utility to facilitate specific outcomes 
10 
that are deemed beneficial to its customers and align with State policy.   
11 
Q.    
What was the Commission’s rationale for establishing an EAM framework 
12 
within the REV Track 2 Order?  
13 
A. 
In the May 19, 2016, REV Track 2 Order in Case 14-M-0101, the 
14 
Commission focused on establishing a “modern regulatory model” that 
15 
challenges utilities to take actions to achieve certain Commission and New 
16 
York State policy objectives by better aligning utility shareholder financial 
17 
interest with consumer interest.  The Commission identified the 
18 
establishment of additional earnings opportunities for utilities, including 
19 
EAMs, as an important component of a modern regulatory model.  As 
20 
noted on page 2 of the REV Track 2 Order, the EAMs are “intended to 
21 
create a regulatory environment where utilities can create shareholder 
22 
1408


--- Page 232 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT 
MECHANISMS PANEL 
7 
 
value…by integrating third-party solutions and capital that improve the 
1 
efficiency, resiliency, and flexibility of the physical networks, reduce 
2 
consumer total costs and achieve the State’s policy objectives.” The 
3 
Commission has identified multiple customer benefits that can be 
4 
achieved from utility actions taken to implement the policy objectives of 
5 
the REV proceeding including: 1) lower customer costs through improved 
6 
efficiency in capital investments; 2) better utilization of existing assets; 3) 
7 
lower energy usage through energy efficiency (“EE”); and 4) reduced 
8 
carbon emissions.   
9 
Q.    
How do Central Hudson’s EAMs benefit its customers?  
10 
A. 
Central Hudson’s EAMs benefit customers in a variety of ways.  Each of 
11 
the proposed EAMs are consistent with the Climate Leadership and 
12 
Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”) and support the State’s clean 
13 
energy policies, many of which deliver benefits to customers (and society 
14 
more broadly) through greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emissions reductions.  
15 
For instance, achievement of the DER Utilization EAM represents a 
16 
significant reduction in GHG emissions by integrating more renewable 
17 
generation onto the utility system that can displace existing fossil fuel 
18 
generation.  Certain EAMs also incentivize Central Hudson to maximize 
19 
utility programs that provide direct benefits to customers.  For instance, 
20 
achievement of the Electric Vehicle Supply Equipment and Infrastructure 
21 
EAM represents successful proliferation of EV charging infrastructure 
22 
1409


--- Page 233 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT 
MECHANISMS PANEL 
8 
 
throughout Central Hudson’s service territory, improving access to vehicle 
1 
charging for all customers, providing incentives to customers who invest in 
2 
vehicle chargers at their site, and directing the highest incentives to those 
3 
within Disadvantaged Communities (“DAC”).  Other EAMs are aligned with 
4 
outcomes that reduce utility costs to customers.  For instance, 
5 
achievement of the Electric Peak Reduction EAM would drive a reduction 
6 
in the Company’s wholesale capacity requirement, which reduces capacity 
7 
costs passed on to customers and could also mitigate the need for load-
8 
driven infrastructure upgrades.  Most of the EAMs deliver some 
9 
combination of these and other types of benefits.   
10 
A. 
Has Central Hudson quantified these benefits?  
11 
Q.    
Yes, Central Hudson has utilized an experienced consultant to complete a 
12 
comprehensive benefit cost analysis for all of its proposed EAMs.  The 
13 
analysis demonstrates that if the Company were to reach maximum 
14 
achievement on each of its EAMs there would be a positive net benefit of 
15 
approximately $516 million and a benefit-cost ratio of 2.50.  The results of 
16 
this analysis are included in more detail within Exhibit __ (EAMP-1). 
17 
IV. 
COMPANY’S EXISTING EAMs 
18 
Q.    
Does Central Hudson currently have EAMs in place?  
19 
A. 
Yes, Central Hudson currently has electric and gas EAMs that the 
20 
Commission approved in its November 18, 2021 Order Adopting Terms of 
21

Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plan (“2021 Rate 
1

2 
Q.    
How many EAMs does the Company currently have in place?  
3 
A. 
As shown in Tables 1 and 2 below, there are ten electric EAMs, two gas 
4 
EAMs, and one combined electric and gas EAM.  The Heat Pump, Electric 
5 
Energy Efficiency, Gas Energy Efficiency, EV Supply Equipment and 
6 
Infrastructure (“EVSE&I”) Level 2, and EVSE&I DC Fast Charger EAMs 
7 
are “Share-The-Savings” EAMs, which have targets and earnings levels 
8 
determined formulaically based on a variety of performance metrics.  The 
9 
DER Utilization, Low-Moderate Income Energy Efficiency (combined 
10 
electric and gas) and EV Adoption EAMs have specific target levels to be 
11 
achieved including minimum, midpoint and maximum performance levels.  
12 
The Electric Peak Reduction and Gas Peak Reduction EAMs are 
13 
determined formulaically based on various inputs.  Central Hudson is 
14 
eligible for pretax earnings adjustments when achieving the targets 
15 
associated with these ten EAMs.  Finally, the Load Factor, Residential 
16 
Energy Intensity, and Commercial Energy Intensity EAMs are tracking-
17 
only EAMs with no targets.  The current EAMs are effective through the 
18 
end of calendar year 2024.  Each EAM and associated target, 
19 
achievements and incentive levels under the 2021 Rate Plan are shown 
20 
within Table 1 and Table 2 below.

V. 
DISADVANTAGED COMMUNITIES ENERGY EFFICIENCY BENEFITS 
11 
Q.    
Please describe the Company’s proposal to modify the Energy Efficiency 
12 
EAMs. 
13 
A. 
Central Hudson is proposing to replace the three existing Energy 
14 
Efficiency EAMs with two Disadvantaged Communities Energy Efficiency 
15 
Benefits EAMs, one for electric and one for gas. 
16 
Q.    
What is a Disadvantaged Community and what is the rationale for this 
17 
EAM? 
18 
A. 
Disadvantaged Communities are communities that bear burdens of 
19 
negative public-health effects, environmental pollution, impacts of climate 
20 
change, and possess certain socioeconomic criteria, or comprise high 
21 
concentrations of low- and moderate-income households.  Pursuant to the 
22 
1417


--- Page 241 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT 
MECHANISMS PANEL 
16 
 
CLCPA, the Climate Justice Working Group (“CJWG”) established criteria 
1 
to identify Disadvantaged Communities in the State.  These criteria were 
2 
adopted on March 27, 2023.  In addition, the CLCPA states that, to the 
3 
extent practicable, spending on clean energy and energy efficiency 
4 
programs, projects, or investments shall be directed in a manner that 
5 
ensures that Disadvantaged Communities receive at least 35 percent, with 
6 
the goal of 40 percent, of such spending.  Central Hudson is proposing 
7 
this EAM to prioritize alignment with this key state policy objective.  The 
8 
EAM would incentivize Central Hudson to explore ways to direct a higher 
9 
proportion of benefits to DACs, including but not limited to targeted 
10 
marketing and recruitment efforts, community events, enhanced 
11 
incentives, and/or unique efficiency offerings.   
12 
Q.    
What is the proposed structure of these EAMs?  
13 
A. 
These EAMs incentivize Central Hudson to direct a higher proportion of 
14 
gas and electric energy efficiency spending to projects located within 
15 
Disadvantaged Communities.  Performance will be based on the 
16 
percentage of spending in the gas and electric Energy Efficiency portfolios 
17 
on efficiency projects located in Disadvantaged Communities as defined 
18 
by the CJWG.  Program level, portfolio level, or general administrative 
19 
costs would be allocated to Disadvantaged Communities in the same 
20 
proportions as direct project costs for the purposes of this EAM.   
21 
1418


--- Page 242 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT 
MECHANISMS PANEL 
17 
 
Q.    
How would the targets be set?  
1 
A. 
Central Hudson proposes that the minimum target be set at 35% of 
2 
spending in each individual portfolio (electric Energy Efficiency and gas 
3 
Energy Efficiency) in accordance with the CLCPA target.  The midpoint 
4 
and maximum targets would be 40% and 45%, respectively.  For 
5 
performance between minimum and midpoint, or midpoint and maximum, 
6 
the Company would earn a linearly prorated share. 
7 
Q.    
Will the EAM performance be aligned with the benefits of energy savings?  
8 
A. 
Central Hudson’s Energy Efficiency Programs have a proven track record 
9 
of delivering cost effective energy savings for our customers.  Through this 
10 
EAM, disadvantaged communities will not only benefit through the direct 
11 
efficiency program spending but will also experience utility cost savings 
12 
and non-energy benefits.  To encourage funds to continue to be spent 
13 
efficiently, Central Hudson proposes that the Commission authorized 
14 
energy efficiency target for the calendar year be considered a threshold 
15 
for achieving the Disadvantaged Communities Energy Efficiency Benefits 
16 
EAM.  For 2025 specifically, the target would be derived based on the 
17 
remaining savings needed to achieve the cumulative 2019-2025 non-LMI 
18 
Energy Efficiency targets in accordance with the methodology directed by 
19 
the PSC within the Commission’s June 23, 2023 Order Approving Funding 
20 
for Clean Heat Program (“Clean Heat Order”) in Case 18-M-0084.  PSC 
21 
authorized targets for years 2026 and 2027 are anticipated as part of the 
22 
1419


--- Page 243 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT 
MECHANISMS PANEL 
18 
 
New Efficiency: New York (“NENY”) Interim Review that is currently 
1 
underway in Case 18-M-0084 and Central Hudson proposes to adopt 
2 
these anticipated targets for the purposes of this EAM.   
3 
VI. 
HEAT PUMP EAM 
4 
Q.    
Is Central Hudson proposing a Heat Pump EAM? 
5 
A. 
Central Hudson developed a proposal for continuation of its Heat Pump 
6 
Share-The-Savings EAM.  However, pursuant to the July 20, 2023 Order 
7 
Directing Energy Efficiency and Building Electrification Proposals in Case 
8 
18-M-0084, the Commission noted that “Staff indicates that the Share-the-
9 
Savings structure of current EE and BE EAM’s, which rewards cost 
10 
savings, does not fully align with the current clean energy goals…” and 
11 
furthermore that the Commission “will not consider EE or BE EAMs 
12 
proposed or included as part of any new rate casing filing or joint 
13 
proposal…” Accordingly, the Panel chose not to include the Heat Pump 
14 
Share-the-Savings EAM proposal in its testimony.   
15 
VII. 
DER UTILIZATION EAM 
16 
Q.    
Please describe the purpose and structure of the DER Utilization EAM. 
17 
A. 
The DER Utilization EAM incentivizes Central Hudson to collaborate with 
18 
third parties to advance the use of DER resources in Central Hudson’s 
19 
service territory.  The DER Utilization EAM provides incentives for 
20 
increased DER penetration which will play a key role in helping New York 
21 
1420


--- Page 244 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT 
MECHANISMS PANEL 
19 
 
State reach its clean energy goals as set forth in the CLCPA.  The DER 
1 
Utilization EAM measures the impact of newly added DER in Central 
2 
Hudson’s service territory.  Performance is based on the sum of the 
3 
annualized MWh generated by DER in Central Hudson’s service territory, 
4 
including large solar Community Distributed Generation and standalone or 
5 
behind the meter electric energy storage resources.   
6 
Q.    
Is the Company proposing any changes to the DER Utilization EAM?  
7 
A. 
The Company is proposing to maintain the structure and computation of 
8 
the DER Utilization EAM pursuant to the 2021 Rate Plan in Cases 20-E-
9 
0428 and 20-G-0429.  However, the proposed targets were updated for 
10 
calendar years 2025, 2026, and 2027.   
11 
Q.    
How were the proposed DER Utilization targets developed?   
12 
A. 
The proposed DER Utilization EAM targets are based on the detailed DER 
13 
forecast that has been developed for the 2023 DSIP, which includes 
14 
multiple DER scenarios.  The Company proposes that its minimum target 
15 
be based on the 50th percentile DER scenario, its maximum target be 
16 
based on the 75th percentile DER scenario, and the midpoint target set 
17 
equally between minimum and maximum.  For performance between 
18 
minimum and midpoint, or midpoint and maximum, the Company would 
19 
earn a linearly prorated share.  More detail can be found in Exhibit __ 
20 
(EAMP-3). 
21 
1421

Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT

Q.    
How do the proposed DER Utilization targets align with achievement of 
1 
the CLCPA targets?  
2

To establish alignment with CLCPA, Central Hudson referred to the 2023 
3 
New York State Independent System Operator (“NYISO”) Gold Book, 
4 
which includes a DER forecast that reflects the level of deployment 
5 
needed to achieve the CLCPA’s solar generation and energy storage 
6 
targets.  Central Hudson’s proposed maximum DER adoption target 
7 
exceeds the achievement level that would result from NYISO’s 2023 Gold 
8 
Book DER forecast, indicating that it is aligned with and/or beyond what is 
9 
needed to achieve CLCPA mandates.  More detail can be found in Exhibit 
10 
__ (EAMP-3).

VIII. 
ELECTRIC PEAK REDUCTION 
13 
Q.    
Please describe the purpose and structure of the Electric Peak Reduction 
14 
(“EPR”) EAM. 
15 
A. 
The EPR EAM incentivizes the Company to reduce its NYISO Zone G-J 
16 
Locality Peak which in turn reduces capacity costs that are passed onto 
17 
customers.  To the extent that there is a decline in the actual weather 
18 
normalized NYISO Zone G-J coincident electric peak below the baseline 
19 
level established for the EPR EAM, the Company will receive an incentive 
20 
under the EPR EAM pursuant to the 2021 Rate Plan. 
21 
1422

Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____

Q.    
Is Central Hudson proposing any changes to the structure of the EPR 
1 
EAM?  
2 
A. 
No, but we are proposing to modify the detailed explanation of the EPR 
3 
metric as included in the 2021 Rate Plan for clarification purposes as 
4 
shown in Exhibit __ (EAMP-4).  The proposed revisions include additional 
5 
text in the performance and measurement sections clarifying that the G-J 
6 
coincident load should be weather normalized to be consistent with the 
7 
existing description of the EAM, as well as adding additional clarity on 
8 
which years’ forecast should be used for each part of the calculation.

IX. 
EV ADOPTION 
10 
Q.    
Describe the structure and purpose of Central Hudson’s EV Adoption 
11 
EAM.   
12 
A. 
The EV Adoption EAM incentivizes the Company to reduce GHG 
13 
emissions by facilitating greater penetration of EVs.  EVs reduce GHG 
14 
emissions relative to traditional internal combustion engine vehicle 
15 
technologies that rely on emissions-intensive fuel sources like gasoline 
16 
and diesel.  The EV Adoption EAM is an outcome-based metric and will be 
17 
measured as the incremental lifetime metric tonnes of avoided CO2 from 
18 
incremental EVs registered in Central Hudson’s service territory.  EVs are 
19 
defined as battery electric vehicles (“BEVs”) and Plug-in hybrid electric 
20 
vehicles (“PHEVs”). 
21 
1423


--- Page 247 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT 
MECHANISMS PANEL 
22 
 
Q.    
Is Central Hudson proposing any changes to the structure of this EAM? 
1 
A. 
No, but we are proposing to update the EV Adoption targets and 
2 
assumptions about per vehicle emissions reductions.   
3 
Q.    
Please describe the updated EV Adoption targets. 
4 
A. 
The proposed targets for 2025, 2026, and 2027 are based on the number 
5 
of BEVs and PHEVs registered in the service territory.  Measurement 
6 
would be based on each year’s data on BEV and PHEV vehicles 
7 
registered in Central Hudson’s territory as obtained from the Atlas Public 
8 
Policy - EvaluateNY website on or about March 31st of the subsequent 
9 
year.   
10 
Q.    
How did Central Hudson develop the proposed targets? 
11 
A. 
The proposed EV Adoption targets are based on the detailed EV forecast 
12 
that has been developed for the 2023 DSIP, which includes multiple EV 
13 
adoption scenarios.  The Company proposes that its minimum targets be 
14 
based on the 50th percentile EV adoption scenario, its maximum targets 
15 
be based on the 75th percentile EV adoption scenario, and the midpoint 
16 
targets set equally between minimum and maximum.  For performance 
17 
between minimum and midpoint, or midpoint and maximum, the Company 
18 
would earn a linearly prorated share.  More detail can be found in Exhibit 
19 
__ (EAMP-5).   
20 
Q.    
How do the proposed EV Adoption targets align with achievement of the 
21 
CLCPA targets?  
22 
1424

A. 
While the CLCPA itself does not contain a specific target for number of 
1 
EVs adopted by year, transportation electrification is a major part of the 
2 
state’s decarbonization strategy.  New York’s Appliance and Equipment 
3 
Efficiency standards4 require that all sales or leases of new light-duty 
4 
passenger vehicles in New York must be Zero Emissions Vehicles 
5 
(“ZEVs”) by 2035.  Within its 2023 DSIP, Central Hudson modeled multiple 
6 
scenarios of EV Adoption based on based on empirical EV adoption data 
7 
derived from vehicle registration data (including vehicle type) for Central 
8 
Hudson's territory since 2018.  Central Hudson’s proposed maximum EV 
9 
Adoption targets are based on adoption levels which surpass the share of 
10 
sales for 2025-2027 that would be projected to align with 100% share of 
11 
new sales by 2035.  More detail can be found in Exhibit __ (EAMP-5).

13 
per vehicle?

15

16 
intensities and other key parameters as obtained from reliable sources.  
17

10

11

12

16

18

19

20 
recognize a more current projection.  Accordingly, and if required, the 
21 
Rate Year 1 allowances will be adjusted.

III. 
DISADVANTAGED COMMUNITIES EAMS 
9 
Q.

10

11 
Benefits EAMs? 
12

13

14

18

PEAK REDUCTION EAMS 
3 
Q.

4

5

6

7 
these EAMs be discontinued because Staff has found that “…the 
8

9

10 
because of unexpected downturns in the economy or other negative 
11

12

17 
The REV Order states: "As a general matter, we reject the arguments of 
18 
parties that would confine EAMs to items under direct control or strong 
19 
influence of the utility, to the extent that 'influence' is interpreted as the 
20 
implementation of specific approved programs."  The Company does not 
21 
believe it is appropriate to discontinue its Electric and Gas Peak Reduction 
22

VII. 
EVSE&I EAMS 
3 
Q.    
Does the Company propose any updates to its proposed EVSE&I EAMs? 
4 
A. 
Yes, since the Company filed its direct testimony, the Commission’s EV 
5 
Order adopted a revised two-component Make-Ready Share-the-Savings 
6 
EAM.  Specifically, the baseline costs were updated to reflect actual cost 
7 
data for completed projects, with unique DC Fast Charger baseline costs 
8 
for each utility and Level 2 baseline costs differentiated between upstate 
9 
and downstate utilities.  Additionally, the EV Order adopted a three-tier 
10 
incentive structure that maintains a maximum 30% share of savings at the 
11 
full plug deployment target achievement level, while adding minimum and 
12 
midpoint share of savings at lower plug deployment achievement levels.  
13 
The Commission expressed that this arrangement incentivizes both 
14 
increased plug deployments and budget management. 
15 
Q.    
Does Central Hudson propose to update the EVSE&I EAMs to be 
16 
consistent with this revised EAM structure? 
17 
A. 
Yes.

BENEFITS TO CUSTOMERS 
19 
Q.    
Did any parties raise concerns about the benefits to customers that result 
20 
from EAMs?  
21 
1444


--- Page 268 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419

15 
 
A.

1

2 
nonexistent."  
3 
Q.    
Does Central Hudson agree with this statement?  
4

5

6

7 
customers.  For instance, achievement of the Electric Peak Reduction 
8 
EAM would drive a reduction in the Company’s wholesale capacity 
9 
requirement, which reduces capacity costs passed on to customers and 
10 
could also mitigate the need for load-driven infrastructure upgrades.  In 
11 
combination, the benefits and costs associated with Central Hudson’s 
12 
proposed EAMs result in a significant net benefit, even when avoided CO2 
13 
benefits are excluded.  More detail on the benefit cost analysis, which is 
14 
consistent with the Commission’s Benefit Cost Analysis Framework 
15 
established in Case 14-M-0101, can be found in Exhibit __ (EAMP-1).

6 
Q. 
Beginning with utility plant and depreciation, please describe the 
7 
information contained in Exhibit __ (ATP-1) which bears the caption “Rate 
8 
Base Noninterest-Bearing Construction Work in Progress”. 
9 
A. 
The amounts of the noninterest-bearing portion of Construction Work in 
10 
Progress (“CWIP”) included in rate base for the historical and forecast 
11 
period are shown in Exhibit __ (ATP-1).  For the historical period on 
12 
Schedule A, the total AFUDC charged to CWIP is divided by the average 
13 
AFUDC rate used for the twelve-month period to arrive at the twelve-
14 
month average interest-bearing CWIP balance.  The twelve-month 
15 
average interest-bearing CWIP balance was then deducted from the 
16 
corporate twelve-month average total CWIP balance to determine the 
17 
average non-interest-bearing CWIP balance for the period.  This same 
18 
method was used to determine the non-interest-bearing CWIP balance for 
19 
the projected period shown on Schedules B and C of Exhibit __ (ATP-1). 
20

10 
Q. 
Would the Panel briefly describe the Company’s method of accruing 
11 
depreciation? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Company’s annual provisions for depreciation are generally 
13 
computed and accrued on a straight-line basis using the half-year 
14 
convention and rates based on estimated average service lives and 
15 
estimated net salvage, with the exception of software which does not use 
16 
the half-year convention, but rather is amortized on a straight-line basis 
17 
over the estimated average service life beginning with the in-service 
18 
month.  A depreciation rate is established for each depreciable plant 
19 
account.  This method is consistent with the method utilized in prior 
20 
Company rate cases, including its last rate cases, 20-E-0428 and 20-G-
21 
0429. 
22 
1491

Q. 
Has the Company reviewed the adequacy of the depreciation factors 
1 
currently being used? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Company had a depreciation study performed by an outside 
3 
consultant, Gannett Fleming, including an analysis of average service life, 
4 
net salvage factors, and retirement dispersion patterns using historical 
5 
data through June 30, 2022 (“Study”). 
6 
Q. 
Please describe this Study. 
7 
A. 
The Study is comparable to the study that was used to set the Company’s 
8 
current depreciation rates and contains the elements for a depreciation 
9 
study as required by the Commission.  See Joint Proposal Appendix V, 
10 
adopted by Commission Order issued and effective November 18, 2021 in 
11 
Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429.  These elements include: 1) rolling and 
12 
shrinking band analyses for each account; 2) the width of the rolling and 
13 
shrinking bands analyzed (not to be greater than ten years and not less 
14 
than five years); 3) the shrinking band analyses starting with all data and 
15 
decreasing to one year of data; 4) statistical results regarding Average 
16 
Service Life of each account including analysis of “h-type” or “Iowa-type” 
17 
curve fitting analysis, the related “fit index” and plots of the observed and 
18 
smoothed survivor curve for each account along with the fitted “h-type” or 
19 
“Iowa-type” survivor curve; and 5) a net salvage study for each plant 
20 
account showing historical gross salvage, cost of removal and net salvage 
21 
for each year of historical data included in the net salvage study along with 
22 
rolling band analysis results, with the width of the rolling band being five 
23 
1492

Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____

13 
 
years.  The consultant’s report associated with the Study indicates that in

factors including Company plans and operating policies and the expert

experienced and estimated by the electric and gas industries.

Q. 
Please explain the manner in which the Company apportions O&M 
17 
expenses that are not directly assignable to electric or gas operations to 
18 
those departments. 
19 
A. 
Expenses not directly assignable to a particular department, but closely 
20 
related to other expenses charged to that department, are distributed in 
21 
proportion to such other expenses.  For example, pension costs and fringe 
22 
benefit costs are related to payroll costs.  They are, therefore, allocated 
23 
1498


--- Page 322 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE ACCOUNTING AND TAX PANEL 
19 
 
between electric and gas operations in a manner that tracks the 
1 
distribution of payroll dollars.  Other O&M expenses that are not 
2 
assignable to a particular department (“common” costs) are apportioned to 
3 
electric and to gas based on a common cost allocation ratio.

Q. 
How are operating taxes apportioned to the Electric Department and Gas 
6 
Department? 
7 
A. 
The Company has apportioned operating taxes between the Electric and 
8 
Gas Departments in a consistent manner and approved in at least the past 
9 
three rate proceedings.  Payroll related taxes are apportioned between the 
10 
electric and gas operating expenses in a manner that tracks the 
11 
distribution of payroll dollars.  Revenue-related taxes are apportioned to 
12 
the departments on the basis of revenues subject to the respective taxes.  
13 
The sales and use tax is charged on the basis of related taxable items.  
14 
The State hazardous waste tax is charged on the basis of related taxable 
15 
items.  Distribution of property taxes to the Electric and Gas departments 
16 
is based on the relative book values of the property within each political 
17 
district.  Property taxes are further categorized into real estate tax and 
18 
special franchise tax. Real estate tax is allocated to “out street” facilities, 
19 
and special franchise tax is allocated to “in street” facilities. 
20

1

3 
taxable wage base, if applicable.
