different period; or 2) permanent differences, which relate to items that are 
4 
recorded for either book or tax purposes, but not for both such purposes.  
5 
An example of a timing difference is the Mortgage Bond Redemption 
6 
Premium, which is deducted from taxable income when incurred, but 
7 
deducted from book income over the life of the new bond.  An example of 
8 
a permanent difference is the business meals and entertainment expense, 
9 
which is fully deductible for book purposes, but only 50% deductible for tax 
10 
purposes.  The 50% not allowed for tax purposes is a permanent 
11 
difference. 
12 
Q. 
Briefly describe how Federal income tax was calculated for the projected 
13 
bridge periods and the Rate Year. 
14 
A. 
The calculation of Federal income tax for the projected period is consistent 
15 
with the method approved in prior Company rate cases, which is on an 
16 
income based tax method and reflects a provision for deferred tax 
17 
expense in accordance with normalization rules under the Tax Reform Act 
18 
of 1986.   
19 
Q. 
Briefly describe how New York State income tax is calculated. 
20 
A. 
The New York State (“NYS”) income tax is calculated based on the 
21 
application of one of three different methods:  1) the income based tax 
22 
method; 2) the fixed dollar minimum method or 3) the capital based tax 
23 
1502


--- Page 326 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE ACCOUNTING AND TAX PANEL 
23 
 
method, which is currently set to expire on  January 1, 2027.  The 
1 
Company’s tax liability is based on the highest of the three different 
2 
methods, which for the Rate Year is capital based tax.  The NYS income 
3 
taxes included in Exhibit __ (ATP-10) and in the development of revenue 
4 
requirements are based on this determination.  A summary of the three 
5 
methods is below: 
6 
1) The income-based tax method starts with Federal taxable income and 
7 
is adjusted to exclude NYS income taxes.  NYS tax law provides for 
8 
adjustments for qualified public utilities for depreciation on transition 
9 
property.  Transition property is property placed in service prior to 
10 
January 1, 2000.  With respect to such property, a deduction is allowed 
11 
for the depreciation expense shown on the books and records of the 
12 
taxpayer rather than the Federal depreciation deduction.  Additionally, 
13 
the amortization of certain regulatory assets, deferred prior to January 
14 
1, 2000, currently deducted on the books, can be deducted from 
15 
income to arrive at NYS taxable income.  These adjustments have 
16 
been reflected in the calculation of NYS income taxes in Exhibit __ 
17 
(ATP-10).     
18 
2) The fixed dollar minimum method is a sliding–scale minimum tax 
19 
based on a corporation’s NYS receipts and is capped at $200,000.   
20 
3) The capital based tax is based on a percentage of average capital and 
21 
is capped at a maximum of $5 million. 
22 
1503


--- Page 327 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE ACCOUNTING AND TAX PANEL 
24

16

17

18

21 
for rate moderation.  After this reclassification, a regulatory liability 
22 
1504

remains and is included in the presentation of projected deferred balances

V. 
ACCOUNTING AND RATEMAKING FOR PENSION AND OPEB  
11 
Q. 
Turning now to other accounting matters, please explain the manner in 
12 
which accounting for Pension and OPEBs have been reflected in these 
13 
filings. 
14 
A. 
The Company’s accounting for Pension and OPEBs are reflected in this 
15 
filing in a similar manner as provided for in the Commission’s November 
16 
18, 2021, Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing 
17 
Electric and Gas Rate Plan in Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429 (“2021 
18 
Rate Plan”) for rate making purposes.  Specifically, the Company follows 
19 
“GAAP” ASC 715 Compensation – Retirement Benefits (“ASC 715”), 
20 
which requires 100% of non-service cost components of pension and 
21 
OPEBs to be reflected as expense and only the service cost component to 
22 
be allocated between expense and capital.  In accordance with this 
23 
1505


--- Page 329 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE ACCOUNTING AND TAX PANEL 
26 
 
accounting, and as approved in Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429, since 
1 
pension and OPEB costs are related to the compensation of employees, 
2 
the annual service costs have been projected to be distributed to expense, 
3 
construction, and other accounts in relation to the normalized distribution 
4 
of payroll costs for the historical period.  The non-service component 
5 
percentage allocation to electric and gas expense is determined 
6 
proportionately based on the normalized distribution of payroll costs 
7 
charged to electric and gas expense.  For regulatory accounting and 
8 
ratemaking, the amounts charged to expense, including a portion of the 
9 
service costs based on payroll distribution and 100% of non-service costs 
10 
components, are recorded in Account 926, Employee Pensions and 
11 
Benefits within the Uniform System of Accounts.  Pursuant to the 
12 
Statement of Policy and Order Concerning the Accounting and 
13 
Ratemaking Treatment for Pensions and Postretirement Benefits Other 
14 
Than Pensions, issued by the Commission on September 7,1993 in Case 
15 
91-M-0890 (“Policy Statement”), the differences between the amounts of 
16 
postemployment benefits included in Company rates and the amounts of 
17 
the postemployment benefit costs charged to expense as determined by 
18 
the Company’s outside actuary, Mercer, under ASC 715 – Compensation 
19 
– Retirement Benefits are deferred as either a regulatory asset or liability, 
20 
as appropriate. 
21

2 
Q. 
Why is there a credit, rather than a cost, associated with these Plans? 
3 
A. 
The actuarial credit is primarily due to the overfunded status of the Plans, 
4 
meaning the amount held within the Plan trust assets exceed the 
5 
projected benefit obligation of the participants. 
6 
Q. 
What is the impact on the Company’s cash flow and financial position as a 
7 
result of incorporating a credit in rates associated with these costs? 
8 
A. 
This has a negative impact on the Company’s cash flow and financial 
9 
position as the credit in rates implies that the Company is supplementing 
10 
its operating cash through an alternate cash flow source associated with 
11 
these plans, such as withdrawing these amounts from the applicable Plan 
12 
trusts, which it does not.   
13

Q. 
Is the Company able to withdraw money from the Plan trusts given the 
1 
overfunded status of the Plans and the impacts of the incorporation of the 
2 
credit into the revenue requirement?  
3 
A. 
There are legal restrictions associated with amounts funded into trusts for 
4 
qualified pension and OPEB plans that limit the Company’s ability to 
5 
withdraw those amounts for purposes other than for the benefit of the 
6 
participants of those Plans.  The Company did identify one potential use 
7 
for excess funding of its VEBA trust associated with the Company’s OPEB 
8 
Plans, which would allow the withdrawal of certain excess funding levels 
9 
to be used for the payment of current medical costs paid by the Company 
10 
associated with active bargaining unit plan participants.  
11 
Q. 
What is the expected impact on the revenue requirement if amounts are 
12 
withdrawn from the VEBA trust? 
13 
A. 
Most notably, the current revenue requirement would be decreased by the 
14 
cash withdrawal, as the cash would be used to pay current medical 
15 
expenses for bargaining unit employees.  Additionally, the withdrawal 
16 
would be expected to increase the actuarial calculation of OPEB expense 
17 
in future periods and to impact the OPEB reserve and related return on 
18 
that reserve, which is incorporated in rate base. 
19 
Q. 
Has the Company made any withdrawals for current medical expenses 
20 
from the VEBA trust at this time?  
21

22 
date other than for the direct payment of OPEB benefits due to the 
23

complex accounting and regulatory mechanisms associated with Pensions 
1 
and OPEBS as defined in Case 91-M-0890 - Statement of Policy and 
2

3

VII. 
PROPERTY TAX METHODOLOGY AND ASSUMPTIONS  
22 
1519


--- Page 343 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE ACCOUNTING AND TAX PANEL 
40 
 
Q. 
What is the general basis upon which property taxes levied upon the  
1 
Company have historically been determined? 
2 
A. 
Property taxes have been based generally on the “market value” of 
3 
property and include taxes on land and the structures and/or equipment 
4 
erected or affixed to the land.  Additionally, utilities also pay special 
5 
franchise taxes, i.e., property taxes on utility equipment located in the 
6 
public right-of-way.  
7 
In New York State, public utility property is valued under a method known 
8 
as the “cost approach.”  Under this approach, the New York State Office of 
9 
Real Property Tax Services (“ORPTS”), and most of the local assessors in 
10 
the Company’s service territory, determine value by using a Reproduction 
11 
Cost New Less Depreciation (“RCNLD”) methodology for utility property.  
12 
RCNLD calculates what it would cost to reproduce property at current 
13 
construction costs based on original cost and a trending index, subtracts 
14 
an allowance for depreciation and obsolescence, if any, and adds the 
15 
value of land to arrive at a “value” for the entire property.  RCNLD is used 
16 
only to value certain of the Company’s structures and all of its taxable 
17 
equipment.  The value of land and office buildings is determined by 
18 
comparable sales data, also known as the “market approach.” 
19 
Q. 
What was the amount of the Company’s property taxes for the Historic 
20 
Year Ended March 31, 2023?  
21 
1520

10 
Q. 
How has Central Hudson realized growth rates greater than the allowed 
11 
2% real property tax cap rate?  
12 
A. 
There are two reasons: (1) the 2% tax cap excludes certain items such as 
13 
large court orders, judgments, and pension growth costs and (2) the 2% 
14 
tax cap assumes a relatively constant property value (i.e., residential 
15 
home).  Unlike a residential homeowner, Central Hudson makes 
16 
significant investments in its infrastructure on an annual basis.  
17 
Accordingly, the assessed values to which the tax rate is applied increase; 
18 
this yields a year-over-year expense growth rate greater than the 2% real 
19 
property tax cap.  
20

21

2 
Q. 
Is the Company familiar with tax adjustments for functional obsolescence?  
3 
A. 
Yes.  Functional obsolescence is the impairment of operating capacity or 
4 
efficiency resulting in a loss in value brought about by (1) the failure of the 
5 
tangible property to meet present or projected needs or (2) where the 
6 
capacity of the tangible property exceeds reasonable anticipated 
7 
demands.  
8 
Q. 
Has the Company sought any property tax adjustment for functional 
9 
obsolescence from any State or local entity during any of the last five tax 
10 
years, including the current tax year?  
11 
A. 
No.  The Company has not filed for functional obsolescence for the period 
12 
noted, as it was determined based on an internal review that the cost of an 
13 
outside expert necessary to file for functional obsolescence would likely 
14 
not be recaptured in tax savings.  This conclusion remains valid in 2023 as 
15 
the base state of the Company’s gas system has not significantly changed 
16 
since the original internal review completed in 2016. 
17 
Q.
